That is the whole idea in one sentence."}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["Everything else in this note is about the word ",["$","em",null,{"children":"carefully"}],". Naively rounding every weight to 4-bit wrecks a large language model. The reason it wrecks it, and the dozen tricks people invented to stop that, is where a whole subfield lives."]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["The thing that took me a while to internalize: quantization is not really about saving disk space. Yes, a 70B model is 140GB in FP16 and about 35GB at 4-bit, and that is why it suddenly fits on your GPU. But the real prize is ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"speed"}],". Generating one token at a time (decode) is bottlenecked on memory bandwidth, i.e. the GPU spends almost all its time reading weights out of memory and barely any time doing math. Make each weight a quarter of the size and you read a quarter of the bytes per token, so decode gets roughly 4x faster almost for free. I unpack that memory-wall argument in ",["$","a",null,{"href":"/notes/LLM-And-Agents/InferenceEgineering","children":"Inference Engineering"}],", so I will not repeat it here."]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"This note is the standalone reference on quantization itself: the math, the algorithms, and the actual tools. Activations are the interesting case. A lot of W8A8 serving uses ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"dynamic per-token"}]," activation quantization for exactly that reason: it sidesteps the risk that your offline calibration data does not match real traffic."]}],"\n",["$","h2",null,{"id":"rounding-nearest-stochastic-and-learned","children":["Rounding: (nearest, stochastic and learned)",["$","a",null,{"href":"#rounding-nearest-stochastic-and-learned","className":"heading-anchor","aria-hidden":true,"tabIndex":"-1","children":"#"}]]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["The obvious choice is ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"round-to-nearest (RTN)"}],": ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-mathml","children":["$","math",null,{"xmlns":"http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML","children":["$","semantics",null,{"children":[["$","mrow",null,{"children":["$","mn",null,{"children":"2.4"}]}],["$","annotation",null,{"encoding":"application/x-tex","children":"2.4"}]]}]}]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-html","aria-hidden":"true","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"base","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"strut","style":{"height":"0.6444em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord","children":"2.4"}]]}]}]]}]," becomes ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-mathml","children":["$","math",null,{"xmlns":"http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML","children":["$","semantics",null,{"children":[["$","mrow",null,{"children":["$","mn",null,{"children":"2"}]}],["$","annotation",null,{"encoding":"application/x-tex","children":"2"}]]}]}]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-html","aria-hidden":"true","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"base","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"strut","style":{"height":"0.6444em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord","children":"2"}]]}]}]]}],". ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-mathml","children":["$","math",null,{"xmlns":"http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML","children":["$","semantics",null,{"children":[["$","mrow",null,{"children":["$","mi",null,{"children":"S"}]}],["$","annotation",null,{"encoding":"application/x-tex","children":"S"}]]}]}]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-html","aria-hidden":"true","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"base","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"strut","style":{"height":"0.6833em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord You cannot delete that noise, you can only choose where it lands. GPTQ, AWQ, SmoothQuant and the rest are just different strategies for steering the noise away from the numbers the model truly depends on."]}],"\n",["$","hr",null,{}],"\n",["$","h1",null,{"id":"why-llms-are-secretly-hard-the-outlier-problem","children":["Why LLMs are secretly hard: the outlier problem",["$","a",null,{"href":"#why-llms-are-secretly-hard-the-outlier-problem","className":"heading-anchor","aria-hidden":true,"tabIndex":"-1","children":"#"}]]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"Quantization is old. People shipped INT8 vision models on phones years before LLMs existed. So why did plain 8-bit break large transformers when it was fine on a ResNet?"}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["The answer, from Dettmers' LLM.int8() work, is ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"outlier features"}],". Once a transformer gets big enough (around 6.7B parameters), a handful of activation dimensions start blowing up to huge magnitudes, sometimes 20x everything around them, and they show up in the ",["$","em",null,{"children":"same"}]," few dimensions for almost every token."]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["Here is why that is so destructive. Remember the scale ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-mathml","children":["$","math",null,{"xmlns":"http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML","children":["$","semantics",null,{"children":[["$","mrow",null,{"children":["$","mi",null,{"children":"S"}]}],["$","annotation",null,{"encoding":"application/x-tex","children":"S"}]]}]}]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-html","aria-hidden":"true","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"base","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"strut","style":{"height":"0.6833em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord mathnormal","style":{"marginRight":"0.0576em"},"children":"S"}]]}]}]]}]," is set by the largest value in the range. One monster value that is 20x bigger than everything else forces a huge step size, and now all the normal-sized weights get squashed into just a few grid slots near zero. Picture pricing a room full of people by net worth, in whole-million-dollar buckets: throw one billionaire into the room and everyone else rounds to \"zero millions.\" The outlier eats all your precision."]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"And you cannot just drop the outliers, because they are load-bearing, the model genuinely relies on them. So this one phenomenon drives most of the algorithm zoo. Every method below is, underneath, a different way to handle outliers:"}],"\n",["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"LLM.int8()"}]," pulls the outlier dimensions out and keeps them in FP16, quantizing the rest."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"SmoothQuant"}]," shifts the outlier magnitude out of the activations and into the weights, which cope better."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"AWQ"}]," protects the specific weight channels that line up with large activations."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"SpQR, QuIP#"}]," either keep the worst weights in high precision, or rotate the whole space so no single dimension is an outlier anymore."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"Hold onto the outlier picture. You are not training on it, you are measuring with it."]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"Turning those measurements into a scale is a real design choice, and there is a ladder from crude to clever:"}],"\n",["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Min-max."}]," Use the literal smallest and largest value. Simplest and most fragile, one outlier sets the range for everyone."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Percentile clipping."}]," Drop the extreme 0.1% first, then take min/max. It detects the handful of outlier dimensions, pulls those specific columns out, and computes them in FP16, while the other 99%+ of the matmul runs in INT8. The two partial results are added back together. So almost all the compute is 8-bit, but the load-bearing outliers never get squashed. This is exactly what ",["$","code",null,{"children":"load_in_8bit=True"}]," does in bitsandbytes. GPTQ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#why-awq-rather-than-gptq","className":"heading-anchor","aria-hidden":true,"tabIndex":"-1","children":"#"}]]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["The one-liner: ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"GPTQ corrects quantization error, AWQ avoids it."}]," Avoiding generalizes better, for three reasons."]}],"\n",["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Calibration overfitting."}]," GPTQ fits second-order structure to your calibration set. Calibrate on one domain and evaluate on another (the paper uses PubMed versus Enron) and GPTQ degrades noticeably while AWQ barely moves, because all AWQ extracted was a per-channel magnitude average, which is a very stable statistic. AWQ needs roughly 10x less calibration data for the same result."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"No act-order tax."}]," GPTQ needs activation-ordering for its best quality, and that breaks the contiguous memory layout fast kernels want. You pick quality or speed."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Cost to apply."}]," Forward passes and a grid search, versus Hessians per layer."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["On raw quality at INT4-g128 the gap is small: on Llama-2-7B, FP16 sits around 5.47 WikiText-2 perplexity, RTN around 5.73, and both GPTQ and AWQ land near 5.60. The separation only becomes decisive at ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"INT3 and below"}],", where RTN falls apart and AWQ holds. Practical read: at 4 bits, choose on kernel support and tooling, not on the paper's table."]}],"\n",["$","blockquote",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"The kernel matters as much as the method."}]," W4A16 has no INT4 tensor core path, so the kernel loads packed INT4, dequantizes to FP16 in registers, and runs FP16 tensor core math. The win is purely bandwidth. At batch 1 you are deeply memory-bound and see ~3x, but a naive INT4 kernel loses its advantage somewhere around batch 8 to 16, because as you drift toward compute-bound the fixed dequant cost stops hiding behind memory stalls. ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Marlin"}]," (and Machete on Hopper) fixes this with better weight layouts and async copies so the speedup survives into the batch 16 to 64 range a real server operates in. In vLLM, an AWQ checkpoint on Ampere or newer dispatches to ",["$","code",null,{"children":"awq_marlin"}]," automatically; if your startup log says plain ",["$","code",null,{"children":"awq"}],", you are leaving throughput on the table."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","h2",null,{"id":"4-smoothquant-move-the-difficulty-from-activations-to-weights","children":["4) SmoothQuant: move the difficulty from activations to weights",["$","a",null,{"href":"#4-smoothquant-move-the-difficulty-from-activations-to-weights","className":"heading-anchor","aria-hidden":true,"tabIndex":"-1","children":"#"}]]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["GPTQ and AWQ are weight-only (W4A16). SmoothQuant goes after the harder prize, full ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"W8A8"}],", where activations are quantized too so you get a genuine integer matmul for compute-heavy serving. The obstacle is those activation outliers again. Weights are flat and easy; activations have the few monster channels. SmoothQuant uses the same scale-shifting trick as AWQ, but aimed the other way: it smooths the activations by pushing some of their range into the weights, which tolerate it It stays cheap because it only trains those few extra parameters, not the model, but it squeezes out more accuracy at low bit-widths than the hand-tuned methods."}],"\n",["$","h2",null,{"id":"going-below-4-bits-spqr-quip-aqlm","children":["Going below 4 bits: SpQR, QuIP#, AQLM",["$","a",null,{"href":"#going-below-4-bits-spqr-quip-aqlm","className":"heading-anchor","aria-hidden":true,"tabIndex":"-1","children":"#"}]]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"At 2 to 3 bits, uniform quantization breaks down and you need cleverer representations. Worth knowing by name and idea:"}],"\n",["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"SpQR (Sparse-Quantized Representation)."}]," Keeps the small set of worst-offending weights in high precision as a sparse side table, and quantizes the rest to 3 to 4 bits. The weight-side version of LLM.int8()'s outlier isolation."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"QuIP / QuIP#."}]," Rotate the weights (with random orthogonal or Hadamard transforms) so no single coordinate is an outlier anymore, spreading the difficulty evenly, which makes even 2-bit viable. QuIP# adds a lattice codebook to pack them tightly. A genuinely different idea: instead of protecting outliers, rotate until there are none."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"AQLM."}]," Represents each weight vector as a sum of vectors from learned codebooks (vector quantization, not scalar). Reaches 2-bit with surprisingly little loss, at the cost of a slower quantization process."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"The through-line: scalar grids run out of room below 4-bit, so you either keep the worst weights high-precision (SpQR), rotate the outliers away (QuIP#), or switch to codebooks (AQLM)."}],"\n",["$","h2",null,{"id":"gguf--llamacpp-k-quants-how-most-people-actually-run-models-locally","children":["GGUF / llama.cpp k-quants: how most people actually run models locally",["$","a",null,{"href":"#gguf--llamacpp-k-quants-how-most-people-actually-run-models-locally","className":"heading-anchor","aria-hidden":true,"tabIndex":"-1","children":"#"}]]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["If you have run a model on your laptop via Ollama or LM Studio, you have used this, because they wrap llama.cpp. GGUF's k-quants group weights into ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"super-blocks of 256"}],", split into sub-blocks that each get their own scale, and then quantize the super-block's scales too (double quantization, to keep the metadata cheap)."]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["The names look cryptic but decode simply. Letting the model learn its own grid is a reliable accuracy win."]}],"\n",["$","h2",null,{"id":"when-qat-is-worth-it","children":["When QAT is worth it",["$","a",null,{"href":"#when-qat-is-worth-it","className":"heading-anchor","aria-hidden":true,"tabIndex":"-1","children":"#"}]]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"QAT needs your full training pipeline, real data, and real compute. So most people should not use it: for \"take an open checkpoint and shrink it,\" PTQ (GPTQ, AWQ, GGUF) is enough, which is why those dominate. QAT earns its cost in three cases: very aggressive bit-widths where PTQ runs out of room, edge/mobile deployment where you control training and want every last point of accuracy, and native-low-precision models like BitNet that are trained ternary from scratch because there is no post-hoc path to 1.58 bits."}],"\n",["$","hr",null,{}],"\n",["$","h1",null,{"id":"kv-cache-quantization","children":["KV-cache quantization",["$","a",null,{"href":"#kv-cache-quantization","className":"heading-anchor","aria-hidden":true,"tabIndex":"-1","children":"#"}]]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"This one gets its own section because at long context it, not the weights, is often what actually runs you out of memory."}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"While generating, you cache the key and value tensors for every past token so you do not recompute them. That cache grows with context length and batch size, and past 100K tokens it can be bigger than the model itself. Quantizing it is how you fit long context and big batches."}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["The wrinkle: keys and values behave differently, so you treat them differently. The finding from KVQuant and KIVI is that the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"key"}]," cache has outlier ",["$","em",null,{"children":"channels"}]," (quantize it per-channel) while the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"value"}]," cache is better behaved (quantize per-token). KIVI pushes this to a 2-bit KV cache. In production the simpler, common option is an ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"FP8 KV cache"}]," (vLLM, TensorRT-LLM), which roughly halves cache memory with almost no quality loss and no fancy logic. Chasing extreme context lengths, the specialized 2-bit methods buy you more, at more complexity."]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["The mental model: weight quantization shrinks the ",["$","em",null,{"children":"model"}],", KV-cache quantization shrinks the ",["$","em",null,{"children":"conversation"}],", and for long-context or high-batch serving the second is frequently the bigger lever."]}],"\n",["$","hr",null,{}],"\n",["$","h1",null,{"id":"what-actually-gets-faster-and-what-does-not","children":["What actually gets faster (and what does not)",["$","a",null,{"href":"#what-actually-gets-faster-and-what-does-not","className":"heading-anchor","aria-hidden":true,"tabIndex":"-1","children":"#"}]]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"This trips people up constantly, so let me be blunt."}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Weight-only quantization (W4A16)"}]," stores weights small but expands them back to FP16 before the matmul, which runs in FP16. So you save memory and speed up ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"memory-bound decode"}]," (fewer weight bytes to stream), but you do ",["$","em",null,{"children":"not"}]," speed up ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"compute-bound prefill"}],", and you add a small expansion cost. This is GPTQ, AWQ, GGUF, NF4. Great for local inference and decode-heavy chat."]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Weight-and-activation quantization (W8A8)"}]," quantizes activations too and runs a real integer (or FP8) matmul on the tensor cores. This speeds up the actual math, so it helps prefill and high-throughput serving. This is SmoothQuant, FP8, INT8 serving in TensorRT-LLM and vLLM. It is harder because of the activation-outlier problem, which is the whole reason SmoothQuant and FP8 exist."]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"So \"I quantized my model, why is prefill the same speed\" has a precise answer: you did W4A16, prefill is compute-bound, and you only touched the memory side. Match the letters (WxAy) to your bottleneck (decode vs prefill) and the confusion goes away."}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"One more reality check: the memory savings are rarely the clean 4x the bit-width suggests. Group scales, zero-points, the odd high-precision tensor, and the KV cache all sit outside the tidy quantized weights. A \"4-bit\" model is usually more like 4.5 to 5 effective bits once you count the metadata."}],"\n",["$","hr",null,{}],"\n",["$","h1",null,{"id":"quantizing-a-real-hugging-face-model","children":["Quantizing a real Hugging Face model",["$","a",null,{"href":"#quantizing-a-real-hugging-face-model","className":"heading-anchor","aria-hidden":true,"tabIndex":"-1","children":"#"}]]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"The theory maps onto a handful of tools you will actually run. ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"TensorFlow Lite"}]," does post-training INT8 (per-axis weights, activations calibrated from a small representative dataset), plus lighter weights-only and FP16 modes. ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"PyTorch"}]," has a full stack: the older eager-mode flow with observers, FX graph-mode quantization, and the newer PT2 export path, running on backends like FBGEMM (server x86) and XNNPACK (mobile ARM). ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"ONNX Runtime"}]," has its own PTQ and QAT tooling. NVIDIA's classic INT8 CNN recipe (the KL-divergence calibration above) came out of exactly this world."]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":"The differences from the LLM case are instructive. Vision models are mostly compute-bound and quantized to INT8 W8A8 to speed up the convolutions, whereas LLM decode is memory-bound and often weight-only. Vision models rarely have the dramatic activation outliers that plague large transformers, so plain per-channel INT8 usually just works without the GPTQ/AWQ/SmoothQuant machinery. If you understand the LLM story, the vision story is the easier special case."}],"\n",["$","hr",null,{}],"\n",["$","h1",null,{"id":"one-table-to-bookmark","children":["One table to maps real numbers onto a grid. Scale sets the spacing, zero-point aligns it, and every method is a smarter way to choose the range or the rounding than plain min-max."}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"The error is noise you cannot delete, only steer. Each bit buys about 6 dB, and the craft is aiming the noise away from the weights the model depends on."}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"LLMs are hard because of a few huge outlier features, and almost every method is a different way to deal with them: isolate them (LLM.int8, SpQR), migrate them (SmoothQuant), protect around them (AWQ), or rotate them away (QuIP#)."}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"GPTQ rounds using second-order information, calibration-only and gradient-free, which is why it is both accurate and fast."}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"Match the WxAy to your bottleneck: weight-only (W4A16) speeds up memory-bound decode, weight-and-activation (W8A8, FP8) speeds up compute-bound prefill and throughput."}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"KV-cache quantization shrinks the conversation, not the model, and at long context it is often the bigger win."}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"QAT teaches a model to survive rounding by training through it with the straight-through estimator, and it is worth the cost only at the aggressive end."}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","p",null,{"children":["And the decision it all collapses to. Serving on GPU at scale, reach for FP8, AWQ, or GPTQ through vLLM or TensorRT-LLM. Running locally, reach for GGUF ",["$","code",null,{"children":"Q4_K_M"}],". Fine-tuning cheaply, reach for QLoRA and NF4. Going below 4-bit, reach for the codebook and rotation methods and expect to work for it."]}]]}],["$","nav",null,{"className":"mt-16 grid grid-cols-1 gap-6 sm:grid-cols-2","children":[["$","$L8",null,{"href":"/notes/Algorithms/AIAlgorithms","className":"block rounded border border-[color:var(--notes-border)] p-4 no-underline transition-colors hover:border-[color:var(--notes-link)]","children":[["$","p",null,{"className":"notes-meta mb-1","children":"← Older"}],["$","p",null,{"className":"notes-card-title text-[17px] leading-snug","children":"AI / ML Algorithms"}]]}],["$","$L8",null,{"href":"/notes/Primers/MultimodalAI","className":"block rounded border border-[color:var(--notes-border)] p-4 text-right no-underline transition-colors hover:border-[color:var(--notes-link)]","children":[["$","p",null,{"className":"notes-meta mb-1","children":"Newer →"}],["$","p",null,{"className":"notes-card-title text-[17px] leading-snug","children":"Multimodal AI"}]]}]]}]]}],["$","aside",null,{"className":"hidden lg:block lg:justify-self-end","children":["$","div",null,{"className":"sticky top-24","children":["$","$Ld",null,{"selector":".notes-article"}]}]}]]}],["$","$Le",null,{"selector":".notes-article"}]]}]
